Rule (

=

6

)

In all branches, the recursive call is
made with at least one argument whose size is strictly smaller than the one of
the input formula. In particular, in the last branch the size of
X
will not
affect the size of the arguments of the recursive call because
X
is a
variable so it cannot add elements to
N
,
N
is a variable itself and
X
is
removed from the recursive call.

Rule (

=

8

)

In the first seven branches, the recursive call is
made with at least one argument whose size is strictly smaller than the one of
the input formula. In the last branch, the recursive call is made with
arguments whose size is equal to those of the input formula. Indeed, we remove

t_{0}
from the l.h.s. set but we add
u(n)
while variable
X
is substituted
by the new variable
N
. However, we now know that
u(n)=s_{j}
for some
j\in[0,k]
. Hence, the recursive call will be processed by one of the first
seven branches which we know reduce the size of at least one of their
arguments.

Rule (

=

9

)

We will prove termination of this rule by
describing a particular abstract implementation.

1.

If the set parts of
A
and
B
are not RIS, then the results of

[
19
]
apply.

2.

If the set part of either
A
or
B
are RIS, then:

(a)

‘Empty’ their domains. This means that if one of the RIS is of the form:

\{\{d_{1},\dots,d_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

then rewrite this set into:

\{u(d_{1})\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{\{d_{2},\dots,d_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

or

\{\{d_{2},\dots,d_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

depending on whether
\phi(d_{1})
holds or not. Continue with this rewriting
until all the
d_{i}
have been processed. This process will transform the
initial RIS into a number of sets of the form:

\{u(d_{i_{1}}),\dots,u(d_{i_{m}})\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

(3)

with
\{d_{i_{1}},\dots,d_{i_{m}}\}\subseteq\{d_{1},\dots,d_{k}\}
and
D

variable or the empty set.

Hence, at the end of this process the domains of the RIS so generated
are either the empty set or a variable.

(b)

If the domain of one of the RIS obtained in the previous step
is the empty set then substitute the RIS by the empty set. Then RIS of
the form (
3
) become

\{u(d_{i_{1}}),\dots,u(d_{i_{m}})\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\emptyset\}
which is equal to

\{u(d_{i_{1}}),\dots,u(d_{i_{m}})\}
.

Hence, at the end of this process the domains of the RIS so generated
are variables.

(c)

Substitute the RIS by new variables. Formally (when at both sides
there are RIS):

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}

(a)

\displaystyle{}\land N_{1}=\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land N_{2}=\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

Note that might be no more RIS at this point, so there is nothing to substitute.

3.

Now apply rule (

=

9

) only to the first conjunct of (
a
) as in

[
19
]
until it terminates, delaying the processing of the remaining
conjuncts in (
a
). As no RIS are in the first conjunct of (
a
)
the results of
[
19
]
apply.

4.

Once rule (

=

9

) applied to the first conjunct terminates,
variables
N_{i}
are substituted back by the corresponding RIS, thus obtaining
equalities of the following forms:

\displaystyle\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

\displaystyle\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{\cdot\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}

\displaystyle\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{\cdot\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

where
D
and
E
are variables (due to the process described in step
2
).

Note that, without RIS, the final formulas returned by (

=

9

) would be
in solved form as their l.h.s. would be variables.

5.

As can be seen, by the form of the returned equalities and by Figure

8
, these equalities are processed by some of the rules added
to deal with RIS.

So (

=

9

) either terminates as in
[
19
]
, or calls other rules
for equality (which is an added behavior compared to
[
19
]
).

Rule (

=

10

)

The recursive call is made with one
argument whose size is strictly smaller than in the initial formula, since
t

is removed from the domain of the RIS.

Rule (

=

11

)

In the first branch there is no recursion
on the same rule because the l.h.s. of the equality constraint is no longer a
RIS but an extensional set. In particular, the size of the arguments are the
same w.r.t. the initial ones. However, the rules that can be fired in this case
(see Figure
8
and the analysis for the corresponding rules), all
reduces the size of at least one of its arguments. In particular, if rules
(

=

9

) or (

=

14

) are called, if they call this rule back, it
will be done with strictly smaller arguments. Then, this loop will run a finite
number of times.

In the second branch the recursive call is made with one argument whose size is
strictly smaller than in the initial formula, since
t
is removed from the
domain of the RIS.

Rule (

=

12

)

The size of the r.h.s. argument of the equality
constraint is strictly smaller than the one of the input formula because
t_{0}

has been removed. Furthermore, the size of
X
will not affect the size of the
arguments of the recursive call because
X
is a variable so it cannot add
elements to
N
,
N
is a variable itself and
X
is removed from the recursive
call.

Rule (

=

13

)

Same arguments of previous rule apply.

Rule (

=

14

)

The recursive call is made with one
argument whose size is strictly smaller than in the initial formula, since
t

is removed from the r.h.s. The size of
D
can affect the size of the arguments
of the recursive call only if
D
is the set part of
A
. However, this case is
processed by one of the following rules: (

=

12

) or (

=

13

).

Rule (

∈

2

)

The recursive call is made with a r.h.s.
argument whose size is strictly smaller than the initial one because
s
is
removed from the set.

Rule (

∉

2

)

Same arguments of previous rule apply.

Rule (

∉

3

)

In either branch the recursive call
is made with a r.h.s. argument whose size is strictly smaller than the initial
one because
d
is removed from the domain of the RIS.

Rule (
17
)

In the first branch the recursive call
is made with a first argument whose size is strictly
smaller than the initial one because
t
is removed from it. In the second
branch, it is both arguments whose sizes are strictly
smaller than the initial ones because
t
is effectively removed from
A
given
that we know that
t\in A
.

Note that in Figure
9
this rule may call rule (
20
),
which in turn may call back this rule or rules (
18
) and
(
19
), thus possibly generating an infinite loop. However, this rule
can call (
20
) only when the first or second
argument is a non-variable RIS. In that case, rule (
20
) removes
one element of the domain of the RIS but it does not necessarily reduces the
size of the arguments. This may fire the callback to
this rule. But this rule reduces the size of its
arguments, so after a finite number of calls the loop finishes. This
shows how the arrow labeled (
\mathit{un}
-a) in Figure
9
can be
traversed only a finite number of times.

See below for an analysis of rule (
20
).

Rule (
18
)

Symmetric arguments to previous rule apply.

Rule (
19
)

In all branches the size of the third argument
of the recursive call is strictly smaller than the initial one because
t
is
removed from it.

Rule (
20
)

Consider the non-variable RIS arguments
of this rule. The rule transforms these RIS into either extensional sets or new RIS.

1.

If all such arguments are transformed into extensional sets, then
the rule does not recur on itself (but calls other rules of the
\mathit{un}

constraint).

2.

If all such arguments are transformed into variable RIS, then the
rule does not recur on itself (but calls other rules of the
\mathit{un}
constraint
or, possibly, is in solved form).

3.

If at least one of such arguments is still a non-variable RIS, then
there is a recursive call but the size of that argument is strictly smaller
than the initial one. In effect, this case occurs when the initial argument in
question is of the following form
\{x:\{a,b\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
with
a\neq b
and
\lnot\phi(a)
. That is, the initial argument is a non-variable RIS
with at least two elements in its domain but the “first” one does not satisfy
the filter. Then,
u(a)
does not belong to the RIS and so it is equal to

\{x:\{b\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. Precisely, this last RIS is used as the
argument to the recursive call. Hence, at least one argument of the recursive
call is strictly smaller than the initial one.

Nevertheless, if
\phi(a)
holds, then
a
is removed from the domain but the RIS
becomes the set part of an extensional set with
u(a)
as its element part. In
this case, the size of this argument is equal to the initial one. However, in
this case, there is no recursive call but a call to one of rules
(
17
), (
18
) and (
19
), which effectively reduce
the size of one of its.

Rule (
24
)

The recursive call is made with a r.h.s.
argument whose size is strictly smaller than the initial one because
t
is
removed from the set.

Rule (
25
)

Symmetric arguments of previous rule apply.

Rule (
26
)

In either branch the recursive call is made
with a r.h.s. argument whose size is strictly smaller than the initial one
because
d
is removed from the domain of the RIS.

Rule (
27
)

Symmetric arguments of previous rule apply.

Therefore, the loops shown in Figure
10
end in a finite number
of times since rules (
24
)-(
27
) reduce the size
of at least one of their arguments every time they are
called.

Local termination of each individual procedure, however, does not guarantee
global termination of
\mathit{SAT}_{\mathcal{RIS}}
, since the different procedures may be
dependent on each other. However, we observe that rewrite rules not involving
RIS in their left-hand sides do not construct any new RIS term in their
right-hand sides. They simply treat RIS terms as any other term. Hence the
presence of RIS terms do not affect their termination, which has been proved in

[
19
]
. Hence, it is enough to consider only the new rules involving
RIS terms.

Rules involving RIS generate both
\mathcal{RIS}
- and
\mathcal{X}
-formulas. The

\mathit{SAT}_{\mathcal{X}}
solver solves the
\mathcal{X}
-formulas without producing

\mathcal{RIS}
-formulas. Then, for each constraint
\pi
and each recursive
rewrite rule for it, we will analyze what
\mathcal{RIS}
-constraints other
than
\pi
are generated. In this way we will see if there are cycles between
rewriting procedures. We proceed by rule inspection.

1.

Rules (

≠

5

), (

≠

6

) and (

≠

7

)
generate
\in
- and
\notin
-constraints.

2.

Rule (

∈

3

) generates
=
-constraints.

3.

Rules (
17
), (
18
) and (
19
) generate

=
-constraints.

4.

Rules (
24
), (
25
), (
26
)
and (
27
), generate
\notin
-constraints.

This is depicted in Figure
11
. Hence, as can be seen, there are no
loops between the rewriting procedures.

\neq
\in
=
\parallel
\notin
\mathit{un}

Calls relation between rewriting procedures

C.4
Equisatisfiability (Theorem
4.14
)

This section contains the detailed proofs on the equisatisfiability of the
rewrite rules involving RIS presented in Sect.
3.2
and Appendix

A
; the
equisatisfiability of the remaining rules has been proved elsewhere

[
19
]
. Hence, these proofs use the rules considering that the control
expression is a variable and that the domain of RIS are not other RIS. These
proofs can be easily extended to the more general case.

In the following theorems and proofs,
\phi
,
\gamma
,
u
and
v
are
shorthands for
F(x)
,
P(x)
,
G(x)
and
Q(x)
, respectively.
Moreover,
note that a set of the form
\{P(x):F(x)\}
(where pattern and filter
are separated by a colon (
:
), instead of a bar (
\mid
), and the pattern is

before
the colon) is a shorthand for
\{y:\exists x(P(x)=y\land F(x))\}
. That is, the set is written in the classic notation for
intensional sets used in mathematics. Finally,
H
denotes the current
hypothesis.

u
and
v
are assumed to be bijective patterns. Recall that all patterns
allowed in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{RIS}}
fulfill this condition. The condition on the bijection of
patterns is necessary to prove equisatisfiability for rule
(
7
).

The proof of Theorem
4.14
rests on a series of lemmas each of
which shows that the set of solutions of left and right-hand sides of each
rewrite rule is the same.

Lemma C.8

(Equivalence of rule (

=

1

))
.

We consider only the following case; all the others covered by this rule
are either symmetric or trivial.

\displaystyle\{x:\emptyset\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\emptyset

Proof C.9
.

\displaystyle\{x:\emptyset\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle=\{u(x):x\in\emptyset\land\phi(x)\}

\displaystyle=\{u(x):\mathit{false}\land\phi(x)\}

\displaystyle=\{u(x):\mathit{false}\}

\displaystyle=\emptyset

Proposition C.10
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall d,&D:\\
&\{x:\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{u(d)\mid\phi(d)\}\cup\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}\end{split}

Proof C.11
.

Taking any
d
and
D
we have:

\displaystyle\{x:\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle=\{u(x):x\in\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\land\phi(x)\}

\displaystyle=\{u(x):(x=d\lor x\in D)\land\phi(x)\}

\displaystyle=\{u(x):(x=d\land\phi(x))\lor(x\in D\land\phi(x))\}

\displaystyle=\{u(x):x=d\land\phi(x)\}\cup\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}

\displaystyle=\{u(d):\phi(d)\}\cup\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}

Lemma C.12

(Equivalence of rule (

=

3

))
.

We will consider only the case where
S\equiv\{c:\textbf{{X}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
with
c\equiv u
, because the other one is proved in
[
19
]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall X,&t_{0},\dots,t_{k}:\\
&X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\textbf{{X}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\\
&\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\textbf{{X}}[X/N]\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\end{split}

where
N
is a new variable.

Proof C.13
.

Taking any
X,t_{0},\dots,t_{k}
we have:

\displaystyle X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\textbf{{X}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by def. of
X
with
a\geq 0
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

[by def.
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\}\cup X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

[by prop. intensional sets]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\cup\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

Now note that if
c\equiv u
, then:

\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\subseteq X

If
\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=X
, then:

\displaystyle X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\cup\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

[by
A=B\cup A\Leftrightarrow B\subseteq A
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\subseteq\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

[by exists
N
completing
\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\cup\{c:N\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\textbf{{X}}[N/X]\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

If
\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\subset X
, then let
N
be the (proper) subset of
X
such that
\{c:N\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. Hence:

\displaystyle X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\cup\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\cup\{c:N\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{s_{1},\dots,s_{a}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\textbf{{X}}[N/X]\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

In the following lemma, the case where
R\equiv X
and
S\equiv X
is covered
in
[
19
]
. Besides, given that the case where
R\equiv\{c:\textbf{{X}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
and
S\equiv X
is covered by the case where
R\equiv\{c:\textbf{{X}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
and
S\equiv\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
, we will prove only the last one.
Indeed, given that
c\equiv u
,
d\equiv v
, we have
\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\subseteq X
. Then, the last case covers the second case when
\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=X
. In fact, in this and the following lemma we will write
\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}
instead of
X
, where
s
denotes zero or more elements;
if
s
denotes zero elements then
\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}
is just
X
.

Lemma C.14

(Equivalence of rule (

=

If

and

:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall X,&t_{0},\dots,t_{m},s_{0},\dots,s_{k}:\\

{}\Leftrightarrow{}&t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}=\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land t_{0}\notin N_{1}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}=\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land t_{0}\notin N_{2}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land t_{0}\notin\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land t_{0}\notin\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land t_{0}\notin N\land t_{0}\notin\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\\

&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land t_{0}\notin\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land\{s_{j}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land s_{j}\notin N\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&{}\quad\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor X=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\land\gamma(t_{0})\\
&{}\quad\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\end{split}\end{split}

Proof C.15
.

\implies)

By H,
t_{0}
must belong the the r.h.s. set. We will divide the proof in the following cases each of which corresponds to one of the branches at the r.h.s. Note that the disjunction of all the preconditions covers all possible cases.

•

t_{0}\in\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}
, then
t_{0}=s_{j}
, for some

–

t_{0}\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\}

*

s_{j}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}

·

First branch:
\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}=\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}=\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land t_{0}\notin N_{1}\land t_{0}\notin N_{2}
.

\displaystyle\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

and

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

Since the last equality is H then the first equality holds.

·

s_{j}=t_{0}

\displaystyle\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

–

t_{0}\in\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\}

Now we consider the sixth branch.

Given that
s_{j}=t_{0}
and that
t_{0}\in\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\}
, then
s_{j}\in\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\}
.

\displaystyle\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by
s_{j}\in\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\}
]

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

•

t_{0}\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}

Now we consider the last branch.

Given that
t_{0}\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}
, then necessarily
t_{0}\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
, then
t_{0}\in X
and
\gamma(t_{0})
holds. Now, the proof is divided in two cases.

–

t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{\textbf{{s}}\}\land\phi(t_{0})

In this case we take
N=X
. Then
\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}=X
, where the last equality holds because
t_{0}\in X
. Now:

\displaystyle\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{\textbf{{s}}\}\land\phi(t_{0})
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
N=X
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by
N=S
]

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

–

t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{\textbf{{s}}\}\lor\lnot\phi(t_{0})

In this case we take
N=X\setminus\{t_{0}\}
, then
X=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}
because
t_{0}\in X
. Besides
t_{0}\notin N
. Now we start from H.

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
X=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}
]

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by properties of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
and intensional sets]

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\cup\{c:\{t_{0}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\quad{}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\cup\{d:\{t_{0}\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by subtract
\{t_{0}\}
on both sides]

\displaystyle(\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\cup\{c:\{t_{0}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\})\setminus\{t_{0}\}

\displaystyle\quad{}=(\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\cup\{d:\{t_{0}\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\})\setminus\{t_{0}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
\setminus
distributes over
\cup
]

\displaystyle(\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})\cup(\{c:\{t_{0}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})

\displaystyle\quad{}=(\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})\cup(\{d:\{t_{0}\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{\textbf{{s}}\}
,
t_{0}\notin N
,
t_{0}\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}
]

\displaystyle\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\Longleftarrow)

We will consider each branch and will prove that the equality holds.

Lemma C.16

(Equivalence of rule (

=

8

))
.

If
c\not\equiv u
and
d\not\equiv v
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall X,&t_{0},\dots,t_{m},s_{0},\dots,s_{k}:\\
&\begin{split}\{t_{0},&\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
{}\Leftrightarrow{}&t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}=\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land t_{0}\notin N_{1}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}=\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land t_{0}\notin N_{2}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{1}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N_{2}\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land t_{0}\notin\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land t_{0}\notin\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:X\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land t_{0}\notin N\land t_{0}\notin\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land t_{0}\notin\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land\{s_{j}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land s_{j}\notin N\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&{}\quad\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor t_{0}=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{j-1},s_{j+1},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\land\gamma(n)\land t_{0}=v(n)\land\phi(n)\land u(n)\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}\\
&\quad{}\land t_{0}=u(n)\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\land\gamma(n)\land t_{0}=v(n)\land\phi(n)\land u(n)=s_{j}\\
&\quad{}\land\{u(n),t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\\
&{}\lor X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\land\gamma(n)\land t_{0}=v(n)\land\lnot\phi(n)\\
&\quad{}\land\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\end{split}\end{split}

Proof C.17
.

The first six branches of this lemma are proved exactly as in the previous
lemma, in both directions, because those branches are equal in both lemmas.
Hence, we will prove only the last three branches, in both directions.

\implies)

In order to prove these branches we need to recall that
u
and
v
are
ordered pairs whose first component is the control variable and whose second
component is a
\mathcal{X}
-term. Then, from now on we will take
u(x)=(x,f_{u}(x))

for some term
f_{u}
and
v(x)=(x,f_{v}(x))
, for some term
f_{v}
.

Besides, all these branches are proved under the assumption as in the previous
lemma:

t_{0}\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}

(4)

The first step in the proof is that we can conclude that
t_{0}
must belong the
the r.h.s. set, by H. Hence, due to (
4
),
t_{0}
must belong
to
\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
, which means that there exists
n\in X
,
\gamma(n)
holds
and
t_{0}=v(n)
. Observe that
t_{0}=v(n)
means that
t_{0}=(n,f_{v}(n))
.

Now, note that the following conditions of the branches under consideration:

\displaystyle\phi(n)\land u(n)\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}

\displaystyle\phi(n)\land u(n)=s_{j}

\displaystyle\lnot\phi(n)

are mutually exclusive. Since
n\in X
then in the first two cases we have
u(n)\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
, which implies that
u(n)
belongs to the l.h.s. set. If
u(n)
belongs to the l.h.s. set of H, then it must belong
to the r.h.s. of H. Then, in the first case we assume
u(n)
belongs to
\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
while in the second we assume is one of the
s_{i}
.

Hence, in order to prove each branch we will assume the corresponding condition
and we will prove the remaining predicates of the conclusion.

•

Assume
\phi(n)\land u(n)\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}
.

Will we prove that
t_{0}=u(n)
by contradiction. We know that
u(n)\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. So:

\displaystyle(n,f_{u}(n))\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land t_{0}\neq(n,f_{u}(n))

[by assumption of this branch]

\displaystyle\implies(n,f_{u}(n))\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land f_{v}(n)\neq f_{u}(n)

[by semant. intensional sets, def. of
v
]

\displaystyle\implies\gamma(n)\land(n,f_{u}(n))=(n,f_{v}(n))\land f_{v}(n)\neq f_{u}(n)

[by drop
\gamma(n)
and equality of pairs]

\displaystyle\implies f_{u}(n)=f_{v}(n)\land f_{v}(n)\neq f_{u}(n)

which is clearly a contradiction. Hence,
t_{0}=u(n)
.

Now we will prove that
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:N\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}
, by dividing the proof in two cases.

–

t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{c:\textbf{{s}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

In this case we take
N=X
. Then
\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}=X
, where the last equality holds because
n\in X
.

*

t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}

\displaystyle\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by
N=X
]

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}
, left absorption]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
X=N
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

*

t_{0}\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. Note that in this case
\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}
.

\displaystyle\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by
N=X
]

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

[by
t_{0}\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
X=N
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

–

t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{c:\textbf{{s}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

In this case we take
N=X\setminus\{n\}
, then
X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}
because
n\in X
. Besides
n\notin N
. Now we start from H.

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}
]

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by properties of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
and intensional sets]

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\cup\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\quad{}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\cup\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by subtract
\{t_{0}\}
at both sides]

\displaystyle(\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\cup\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\})\setminus\{t_{0}\}

\displaystyle\quad{}=(\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\cup\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\})\setminus\{t_{0}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
\setminus
distributes over
\cup
]

\displaystyle(\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})\cup(\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})

\displaystyle\quad{}=(\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})\cup(\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
(\dagger)
]

\displaystyle\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

The justification of
(\dagger)
is the following:

1.

Note that
\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{u(n)\}=\{t_{0}\}

[by assumption of this branch and because in this branch
u(n)=t_{0}
]
, then
\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}=\emptyset
.

2.

\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=\{t_{0}\}

[by
\gamma(n)\land t_{0}=v(n)
]
, then
\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}=\emptyset
.

3.

t_{0}\notin\{c:N\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
because if it does then there exists
n^{\prime}\in N
such that
\phi(n^{\prime})
holds and
t_{0}=u(n^{\prime})
. But,
t_{0}=u(n^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow t_{0}=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))
. On the other hand
t_{0}=(n,f_{v}(n))
and so
(n,f_{v}(n))=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))
which implies that
n=n^{\prime}
. But this is impossible because we have
n^{\prime}\in N
and
n\notin N
.

4.

\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by assumption:
t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{c:\textbf{{s}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
; and by
t_{0}\notin\{c:N\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
]
.

5.

\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by
t_{0}\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}
and
n\notin N
]
.

•

Assume
\phi(n)\land u(n)=s_{j}
.

We have to prove that
\{u(n),t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}
. We proceed as with the previous branch. In fact, the first case is identical (note that there we used assumptions that are also available in this branch). In the second case, we take the same value for
N
and start from H as in the previous branch.

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}
]

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}},n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by properties of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
and intensional sets and
\phi(n)
holds]

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{u(n)\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

\displaystyle\quad{}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\cup\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle\{u(n),t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\cup\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by subtract
\{t_{0}\}
at both sides]

\displaystyle\{u(n),t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}

\displaystyle\quad{}=(\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\cup\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\})\setminus\{t_{0}\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
\setminus
distributes over
\cup
]

\displaystyle\{u(n),t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}

\displaystyle\quad{}=(\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})\cup(\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\setminus\{t_{0}\})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
(\dagger)
]

\displaystyle\{u(n),t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

The justification of
(\dagger)
is the following:

1.

\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=\{t_{0}\}

[by
\gamma(n)\land t_{0}=v(n)
]
, then
\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}=\emptyset
.

2.

t_{0}\neq u(n)
because if not then
t_{0}=s_{j}
which is in contraction with (
4
).

3.

t_{0}\notin\{c:N\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
because if it belongs then there exists
n^{\prime}\in N
such that
\phi(n^{\prime})
holds and
t_{0}=u(n^{\prime})
. But,
t_{0}=u(n^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow t_{0}=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))
. On the other hand
t_{0}=(n,f_{v}(n))
and so
(n,f_{v}(n))=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))

n=n^{\prime}
. But this is impossible because we have
n^{\prime}\in N
and

4.

\{u(n),t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}=\{u(n),t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by assumption:
t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\}\cup\{c:\textbf{{s}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
; by

]

\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\setminus\{t_{0}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by
t_{0}\notin\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\}
and
n\notin N
]
.

•

Assume
\lnot\phi(n)
.

We have to prove that
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}
.We proceed as in the first branch considered in this lemma. In fact, the first case is identical (note that there we used assumptions that are also available in this branch). In the second case, we take the same value for
N
and proceed in the same way. The only difference is in the first item of the justification of
(\dagger)
because now we have
\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\emptyset
because
\lnot\phi(n)
.

\Longleftarrow)

We will consider only the last three branches and will prove that the equality
holds.

Note that when
\phi(n)
holds then
\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{u(n)\}
; and when
\phi(n)
does not hold then
\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\emptyset
.

•

First branch.

\displaystyle\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
; property of intensional sets]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0}\}\cup\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\cup\{t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

\displaystyle=\{t_{0}\}\cup\{c:\{n\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\cup\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\}

\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}

\displaystyle=\{s_{0},\dots,s_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}

The equivalence of rule (

=

9

) has been proved previously for any finite
set (extensional or intensional)
[
19
]
.

Lemma C.18

(Equivalence of rule (

=

10

))
.

We consider only the following case because the others covered by this rule are
similar.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall d,&D:\\
&\begin{split}&\{x:\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\emptyset\\
&\Leftrightarrow\lnot\phi(d)\land\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\emptyset\end{split}\end{split}

Proof C.19
.

Taking any
d
and
D
we have:

\displaystyle\{x:\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\emptyset

[by Prop.
C.10
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d):\phi(d)\}\cup\{u(x):x\in D\land\phi(x)\}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d):\phi(d)\}=\emptyset\land\{u(x):x\in D\land\phi(x)\}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\lnot\phi(d)\land\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\emptyset

Lemma C.20

(Equivalence of rule (

=

11

))
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall d,&D,B:\\
&\begin{split}&\{x:\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B\\
&\begin{split}\Leftrightarrow&\phi(d)\land\{u(d)\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=B\\
&\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B\end{split}\end{split}\end{split}

Proof C.21
.

Taking any
d
,
D
and
B
we have:

\displaystyle\{x:\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B

[by Prop.
C.10
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d):\phi(d)\}\cup\{u(x):x\in D\land\phi(x)\}=B

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d):\phi(d)\}\cup\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B

Now assume
\phi(d)
, then:

\displaystyle\{u(d):\phi(d)\}\cup\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d)\}\cup\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d)\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=B

Now assume
\lnot\phi(d)
, then:

\displaystyle\{u(d):\phi(d)\}\cup\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B

\displaystyle\emptyset\cup\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B

\displaystyle\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=B

which finishes the proof.

Remark C.22
.

Note that in Theorem
C.20
when
B
is:

•

An extensional set of the form
\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}
, then the equality in
the first disjunct becomes an equality between two extensional sets:

\{u(d)\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

which is solved by the rules described in
[
19
]
. In turn, the
equality in the second disjunct becomes an equality between a RIS and an
extensional set:

\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

This equality is managed by either rule (

=

11

) itself (if
D
is
not a variable) or by rule (

=

14

) (if
D
is a variable).

•

A non-variable RIS of the form
\{x:\{e\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
,
then the equality in the first disjunct becomes an equality between an
extensional set and a non-variable RIS:

\{u(d)\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{x:\{e\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

which is managed again by rule (

=

11

). In turn, the equality in the
second disjunct becomes an equality between a RIS and a non-variable RIS:

\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{x:\{e\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

which is managed by the same rule once more.

Moreover, note that in this case there can be up to four cases (and thus up to
four solutions) considering all the possible combinations of truth values of

\phi
and
\gamma

•

A variable RIS of the form
\{x:E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
, then the equality
in the first disjunct becomes an equality between an extensional set and a
variable RIS:

\{u(d)\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}=\{x:E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

which is managed by rule (

=

14

). In turn, the equality in the second
disjunct becomes an equality between a RIS and a variable RIS:

\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{x:E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

which is no further processed (if
D
is a variable) or is processed by rule
(

=

11

) again (if
D
is not a variable).

∎

For the following rule we only consider the case where
S\equiv\{c:\textbf{{X}}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
because the other one covered by the rule corresponds to results
presented in
[
19
]
. In this and the following lemma we will write

\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}
instead of
X
, where
s
denotes zero or more
elements; if
s
denotes zero elements then
\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}
is just

X
.

Lemma C.23

(Equivalence of rule (

=

12

))
.

If
c\equiv u
and
d\equiv v
:

\displaystyle\forall X,t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}:

\displaystyle\quad\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

\displaystyle\quad{}\Leftrightarrow X=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\land\gamma(t_{0})\land\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

Proof C.24
.

In order to simplify the proof we will not write
u
and
v
in the
intensional sets because
c\equiv u
and
d\equiv v
.

\implies)

Clearly,
t_{0}
must belong to
\{d:X\mid\gamma\}
, then
t_{0}\in X
and
\gamma(t_{0})
holds.

If
t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{\textbf{{s}}\}\land\phi(t_{0})
we take
N=X
.
In this case we have:
\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}=X
because
t_{0}\in X
. Now we prove the the last conjunct:

•

t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}

\displaystyle\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by
\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}
and
X=N

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{d:X\mid\gamma\}

[by
X=N
]

\displaystyle=\{d:N\mid\gamma\}

•

t_{0}\in\{\textbf{{s}}\}
. Note that in this case

because

holds by assumption.

\displaystyle\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by

\phi(t_{0})

X=N

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{d:X\mid\gamma\}

[by
X=N
]

\displaystyle=\{d:N\mid\gamma\}

If
t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{\textbf{{s}}\}\lor\lnot\phi(t_{0})
we take
N

to be
X\setminus\{t_{0}\}
. Then,

. Besides,
t_{0}\notin N
. Now we will prove the last conjunct by double inclusion.

Take any
x\in\{d:N\mid\gamma\}
, then
x\in N
and
\gamma(x)
holds. Hence,
x\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\}
because
N\subseteq X

[by construction]
. Now

[by H]
. If
x=t_{0}

then there is a contradiction with the fact that
x\in N
because
t_{0}\notin N
. So
x
cannot be
t_{0}
. If
x=t_{i}
(
i\neq 0
), then
x
trivially
belongs to
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}
. Finally, if

x\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\}
, then
\phi(x)
holds. But
x\in N
so
x\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}
.

Now the other inclusion. Take any
x\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}\}
. If
x=t_{i}
, then
x\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by H]
, which
implies
x\in X
and
\gamma(x)
holds. Given that
x\neq t_{0}

[by
t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}
]
and
x\in X
, then
x\in N

[by construction]
. Since
x\in N
and
\gamma(x)
holds, then
x\in\{d:N\mid\gamma\}
. If
x\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}
then
\phi(x)
holds and
x\neq t_{0}

[by assumption]
. Now
x\in\{t_{0},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by
N\subseteq X
]
. Then, by H,
x\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\}
which means that
x\in X
and
\gamma(x)

holds. Since
x\neq t_{0}
, then
x\in X

x\in N
. Then

.

\Longleftarrow)

\displaystyle\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}},t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}\}

\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}

]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0}\}\cup\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0}\}\cup\{d:N\mid\gamma\}

[by semantics
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{d:N\mid\gamma\}

\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
and properties of intensional sets]

\displaystyle=\{d:X\mid\gamma\}

Lemma C.25

(Equivalence of rule (

=

13

))
.

If
c\not\equiv u
and
d\not\equiv v
:

\displaystyle\forall X,t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}:

\displaystyle\quad\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

\displaystyle\quad{}\Leftrightarrow X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\land\gamma(n)\land t_{0}=v(n)\land(\phi(n)\implies t_{0}=u(n))

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\land\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

Proof C.26
.

In order to prove this lemma we need to recall that
u
and
v
are ordered
pairs whose first component is the control variable and whose second component
is an
\mathcal{X}
-term. Then, from now on we will take
u(x)=(x,f_{u}(x))
for some
term
f_{u}
and
v(x)=(x,f_{v}(x))
, for some term
f_{v}
.

\implies)

Clearly,
t_{0}
must belong to
\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
, which means that there
exists
n\in X
,
\gamma(n)
holds and
t_{0}=v(n)
. Observe that
t_{0}=v(n)

means that
t_{0}=(n,f_{v}(n))
.

Now will we prove that
\phi(n)\implies t_{0}=u(n)
. So assume,
\phi(n)
holds
but
t_{0}\neq u(n)
:

\displaystyle\phi(n)\land t_{0}\neq u(n)

[by
n\in X
and definitions of
t_{0}
and
u
]

\displaystyle\implies(n,f_{u}(n))\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land(n,f_{v}(n))\neq(n,f_{u}(n))

[by H:
\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\subseteq\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
]

\displaystyle\implies(n,f_{u}(n))\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land f_{v}(n)\neq f_{u}(n)

[by semantics intensional sets and def. of
v
]

\displaystyle\implies\gamma(n)\land(n,f_{u}(n))=(n,f_{v}(n))\land f_{v}(n)\neq f_{u}(n)

[by drop
\gamma(n)
and equality of pairs]

\displaystyle\implies f_{u}(n)=f_{v}(n)\land f_{v}(n)\neq f_{u}(n)

which is clearly a contradiction. Hence, if
\phi(n)
holds then
t_{0}=u(n)
.

Finally, we will prove
\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}
by distinguishing two cases. In the first case, we
assume
t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
, and so we
take
N=X
which guarantees that
X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}

[by
X=N
and
n\in X
]
. Now we prove the set equality (last conjunct):

•

t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}

\displaystyle\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by
N=X
]

\displaystyle=\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
t_{0}\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}
, left absorption]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
X=N
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

•

t_{0}\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. Note that in this case

\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}
.

\displaystyle\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by
N=X
]

\displaystyle=\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

[by
t_{0}\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by
X=N
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

In the second case, we assume
t_{0}\notin\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\}\cup\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
, and so we take
N=X\setminus\{n\}
. This means that
X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}

[by
n\in X
]
and
n\notin N
.

Now we proceed by double inclusion. Take any
(x,f_{v}(x))\in\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
, then
x\in N
and
\gamma(x)
holds. As
x\in N
then
x\neq n
and so

(x,f_{v}(x))\neq(n,f_{v}(n))=t_{0}
. Given that
N\subseteq X
, then

(x,f_{v}(x))\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
and so
(x,f_{v}(x))\in\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by H]
. Given that
(x,f_{v}(x))\neq t_{0}
, then
(x,f_{v}(x))\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}
. But
x\neq n
and so if
(x,f_{v}(x))\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
then it actually belongs to
\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
.
Hence
(x,f_{v}(x))\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}
.

Now the other inclusion. Take any
x\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}
. If
x=t_{i}
(for some
i\neq 0
) then
x\neq t_{0}
,
and
x\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by H]
. So we have
n^{\prime}\in X
such that
\gamma(n^{\prime})

holds and
x=(n^{\prime},f_{v}(n^{\prime}))
. Now if
n^{\prime}=n
then we have
x=(n^{\prime},f_{v}(n^{\prime}))=(n,f_{v}(n))=t_{0}
, which is a contradiction with
x\neq t_{0}
. Hence,
n^{\prime}

must be different from
n
and so
n^{\prime}\in N

[by
n^{\prime}\in X=\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}

[by construction] and
n^{\prime}\neq n
]
. Since
n^{\prime}\in N
and
\gamma(n^{\prime})
holds we have

x=(n^{\prime},f_{v}(n^{\prime}))\in\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
. Finally, if
x\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
then
x=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))
with
n^{\prime}\in\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}
and
\phi(n^{\prime})
holds. If
n^{\prime}=n
then
n^{\prime}\in\{\textbf{{s}}\}
because
n\notin N
, and in this case we would have
t_{0}\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
because

\phi(n^{\prime})
holds, but this a contradiction with the assumption. Hence
n^{\prime}\neq n
. On the other hand,
x\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
implies that

x=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))\in\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

[by
N\subseteq X
]
, which in turn implies
x=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))\in\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by H]
.
Then
n^{\prime}\in X
and
\gamma(n^{\prime})
holds. Now
n^{\prime}\in X
iff
n^{\prime}\in\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}

[by construction]
, but we have proved that
n^{\prime}\neq n
, so actually
n^{\prime}\in N
.
Therefore, we have
n^{\prime}\in N
and
\gamma(n^{\prime})
holds so we have
x=(n^{\prime},f_{u}(n^{\prime}))\in\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}
.

\Longleftarrow)

\displaystyle\{d:X\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{d:\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
and properties of intensional sets]

\displaystyle=\{d:\{n\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\cup\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0}\}\cup\{d:N\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0}\}\cup\{t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by left absorption]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{t_{0}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

[by
v(x)=(x,f_{v}(x))
and H:
v(n)=t_{0}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{(n,f_{v}(n))\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

[by H:
\phi(n)\implies u(n)=t_{0}
; see (
\dagger
) below]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{n,\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

[by H]

\displaystyle=\{t_{0},t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}X\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\}

(
\dagger
)   Note that if
\phi(n)
does not hold then

\{c:\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
; and if
\phi(n)
holds then
\{c:\{n\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{(n,f_{u}(n))\}\cup\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{t_{0}\}\cup\{c:\{\textbf{{s}}\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}N\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. Besides if
\phi(n)
holds then
f_{u}(n)=f_{v}(n)
because
t_{0}=v(n)=u(n)
.

Lemma C.27

(Equivalence of rule (

=

14

))
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall&D,y,A:\\
&\begin{split}&\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}\\
&\Leftrightarrow\exists d,E:D=\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}\land\phi(d)\land y=u(d)\land\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=A\end{split}\end{split}

Proof C.28
.

\implies)

By H,
y\in\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
; then:

\exists d:d\in D\land\phi(d)\land u(d)=y

(
\exists
1)

Then we have proved that
\phi(d)\land u(d)=y
holds.

It remains to be proved the existence of
E
and
\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=A
. To this end, the proof is divided in two cases.

In the first case we assume
y\notin A
. Hence, take
E=D\setminus\{d\}
, which verifies
D=\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}
. We will prove
\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=A
by proving that:

\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\subseteq A\land A\subseteq\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

•

\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\subseteq A
. Take any
w\in\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
; then

\exists a:a\in E\land\phi(a)\land u(a)=w

(
\exists
2)

As
D=\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}
then
a\in D
which implies
w\in\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
which implies
w\in\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}
, by H. Since
u
is a bijective
pattern, then
u(a)\neq u(d)
because
a\neq d
because
a\in E=D\setminus\{d\}
. Given that
u(d)=y

[by (

∃

1
)]
and
u(a)=w

[by (

∃

2
)]
, then
w\neq y
, which implies that
w\in A
.

•

A\subseteq\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. Take any
w\in A
;
then, by assumption of this case,
w\neq y

(*)
and
w\in\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
. Hence:

\exists a:a\in D\land\phi(a)\land u(a)=w

(
\exists
3)

So we need to prove that
a\in E
, which by (

∃

3
) will imply that
w\in\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}
, which will prove this branch.

Given that
D=\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}
and that
a\in D
, then
a\in E
iff
a\neq d
. If
a=d
then
u(a)=u(d)
, then
w=y
because
u(a)=w

[by (

∃

3
)]
and
u(d)=y

[by (

∃

1
)]
. And if
w=y
then there is a contradiction with
(*)
.
Therefore,
a\neq d
and so
a\in E
.

In the second case we assume
y\in A
. Hence, take
E=D
, then
\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}=\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}=D
because
d\in D
. If
y\in A
, then
\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}=A

[by semantics of
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]
. So, by H, we have:

\displaystyle\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}=A
]

\displaystyle\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=A

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

[by
D=E
]

\displaystyle\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=A

\Longleftarrow)

By H, let
d
and
E
be such that:

D=\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}\land\phi(d)\land u(d)=y\land\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=A

(5)

Now:

\displaystyle\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{x:\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

[by
D=\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}E\}
in (
5
)]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d):\phi(d)\}\cup\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

[by Lemma
C.10
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{u(d)\}\cup\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

[by
\phi(d)
in (
5
)]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{y\}\cup A=\{y\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}A\}

[by
\{x:E\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}=A
in (
5
)]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathit{true}

The equivalence of the rule for
\neq
(i.e., rule (
8
)) is trivial
because this rule applies the definition of set disequality which is given in
terms of
\in
and
\notin
.

Lemma C.29

(Equivalence of rule (

∈

4

))
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall&D,y:\\
&y\in\{x:D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\Leftrightarrow\exists d:d\in D\land\phi(d)\land y=u(d)\end{split}

Proof C.30
.

The proof is trivial since this is the definition of set membership w.r.t. an
intensional set.

Lemma C.31

(Equivalence of rule (

∉

3

))
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall&t,d,D:\\
&t\notin\{\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\\
&\begin{split}\Leftrightarrow&\phi(d)\land t\neq u(d)\land t\notin\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\\
&\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land t\notin\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\end{split}\end{split}

Proof C.32
.

\displaystyle t\notin\{\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall x:x\in\{d\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}D\}\land\phi(x)\land t\neq u(x)

[by RIS semantics]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Leftrightarrow\forall x:&\phi(d)\land t\neq u(d)\land x\in D\land\phi(x)\land t\neq u(x)\\
&\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land x\in D\land\phi(x)\land t\neq u(x)\end{split}

[by single out
d
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Leftrightarrow&\phi(d)\land t\neq u(d)\land\forall x:x\in D\land\phi(x)\land t\neq u(x)\\
&\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land\forall x:x\in D\land\phi(x)\land t\neq u(x)\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Leftrightarrow&\phi(d)\land t\neq u(d)\land t\notin\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\\
&\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land t\notin\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\end{split}

Concerning the
\mathit{un}
constraint, since rules
(
13
)-(
19
) are extensions of the rules presented by

[
19
]
, the corresponding proofs are trivial. Rule (
20
) is
the only one that truly processes non-variable RIS.

Lemma C.33

(Equivalence of rule (
20
))
.

If at least one of
A,B,C
is not a variable nor a variable-RIS:

\displaystyle\mathit{un}(A,B,C)\Leftrightarrow\mathit{un}(\mathcal{S}(A),\mathcal{S}(B),\mathcal{S}(C))\land\mathcal{C}(A)\land\mathcal{C}(B)\land\mathcal{C}(C)

where
\mathcal{S}
is a set-valued function
and
\mathcal{C}
is a constraint-valued function

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}N_{\sigma}&\text{if $\sigma\equiv\{\{d\sqcup D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}$}\\
\sigma&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}N_{\sigma}=(\{u(d)\sqcup\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\land\phi(d))&\text{if $\sigma\equiv\{\{d\sqcup D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}$}\\
\quad{}\lor(N_{\sigma}=\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land\lnot\phi(d))&\\
\mathit{true}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Proof C.34
.

Actually, this rule covers several cases that could have been written as different rules. Indeed, this rule applies whenever at least one of its arguments is a non variable-RIS. This means that there are seven cases where this rule is applied.

We will prove the equivalence for one of this seven cases because all the other are proved in a similar way. The case to be considered is the following:

\mathit{un}(\{\{d\sqcup D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\},B,\{\{e\sqcup E\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\})

(6)

that is, the first and third arguments are non-variable RIS while the second is a variable.

In this case the rule rewrites (
6
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathit{un}(\mathcal{S}(\{\{d\sqcup D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}),\mathcal{S}(B),\mathcal{S}(\{\{e\sqcup E\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}))

\displaystyle{}\quad\land\mathcal{C}(\{\{d\sqcup D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\})

\displaystyle{}\quad\land\mathcal{C}(B)

\displaystyle{}\quad\land\mathcal{C}(\{\{e\sqcup E\}\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\})\Leftrightarrow

[by applying the definition of
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
]

\displaystyle\mathit{un}(N_{1},B,N_{2})

\displaystyle{}\quad\land(N_{1}=\{u(d)\sqcup\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\}\land\phi(d)\lor N_{1}=\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\land\lnot\phi(d))

\displaystyle{}\quad\land\mathit{true}

\displaystyle{}\quad\land(N_{2}=\{v(e)\sqcup\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\}\land\gamma(e)\lor N_{2}=\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\land\lnot\gamma(e))

Hence, after performing some trivial simplifications we have:

\displaystyle\phi(d)\land\gamma(e)\land\mathit{un}(\{u(d)\sqcup\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\},B,\{v(e)\sqcup\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\})

\displaystyle\lor

\displaystyle\phi(d)\land\lnot\gamma(e)\land\mathit{un}(\{u(d)\sqcup\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\},B,\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\})

\displaystyle\lor

\displaystyle\lnot\phi(d)\land\gamma(e)\land\mathit{un}(\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\},B,\{v(e)\sqcup\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\}\})

\displaystyle\lor

\displaystyle\lnot\phi(d)\land\lnot\gamma(e)\land\mathit{un}(\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\},B,\{E\mid\gamma\mathbin{\bullet}v\})

That is,
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{C}
simply transform each non variable-RIS into an extensional set or another RIS depending on whether the ‘first’ element of each domain is true of the corresponding filter or not. In this way, a disjunction covering all the possible combinations is generated.

As with union, the only rules for
\mathit{disj}
that truly deal with non trivial RIS terms are (
26
) and (
27
). However, given that they are symmetric, we only prove the equivalence for the first one.

Lemma C.35

(Equivalence of rule (
26
))
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}A\parallel&\{\{d\sqcup D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\Leftrightarrow\\
&\phi(d)\land u(d)\notin A\land A\parallel\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land A\parallel\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\end{split}

Proof C.36
.

\displaystyle A\parallel\{\{d\sqcup D\}\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow A\parallel(\{u(d)\mid\phi(d)\}\cup\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\})

[by Lemma
C.10
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow A\parallel\{u(d)\mid\phi(d)\}\land A\parallel\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}

[by distribution]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\phi(d)\land A\parallel\{u(d)\}\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land A\parallel\emptyset)\land A\parallel\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}

[by singleton comprehension]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\phi(d)\land u(d)\notin A\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land A\parallel\emptyset)\land A\parallel\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}

[by disjointness singleton]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\phi(d)\land u(d)\notin A\land A\parallel\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}

[by distribution]

\displaystyle{}\quad\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land A\parallel\{u(x)\mid x\in D\land\phi(x)\}

[by RIS semantics]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\phi(d)\land u(d)\notin A\land A\parallel\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}\lor\lnot\phi(d)\land A\parallel\{D\mid\phi\mathbin{\bullet}u\}

The last theorems concern the specialized rules for RUQ given in Figure
7
. As can be seen the only one that does not have a trivial proof is the following.

Lemma C.37

(Equivalence of rule (
40
))
.

\displaystyle\forall t,A:t\notin A\implies

\displaystyle\quad\{t\sqcup A\}\cup\{x:\{t\sqcup A\}\mid\phi\}=\{x:\{t\sqcup A\}\mid\phi\}\Leftrightarrow\phi(t)\land A\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}=\{x:A\mid\phi\}

Proof C.38
.

First note that

t\notin A\implies\{t\}\parallel A\land\{t\}\parallel\{A\mid\phi\}

(7)

and

\{x:\{t\}\mid\phi\}\subseteq\{t\}

(8)

and

\{x:\{t\}\mid\phi\}=\{t\}\Leftrightarrow\phi(t)

(9)

\displaystyle\{t\sqcup A\}\cup\{x:\{t\sqcup A\}\mid\phi\}=\{x:\{t\sqcup A\}\mid\phi\}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{t\}\cup A\cup\{x:\{t\}\mid\phi\}\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}=\{x:\{t\}\mid\phi\}\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}

[by Lemma
C.10
; semantics
\mathbin{\scriptstyle\sqcup}
]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\{t\}\cup A\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}=\{x:\{t\}\mid\phi\}\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}

[by (
8
);
\{t\}
in left-hand side]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\phi(t)\land\{t\}\cup A\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}=\{t\}\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}

[by (
9
)]

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\phi(t)\land A\cup\{x:A\mid\phi\}=\{x:A\mid\phi\}

[by (
7
); basic property disjointedness and union]
